TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Convicted of embezzling millions of dollars from 1Malaysia Development Berhad, a state investment fund, Najib has slimed his way into royal pardons with a tenacity that would be admirable if it were used for another endeavor. The move has also enraged reformers in Malaysia who had pinned hope on the current prime minister, Anwar Ibrahim, to clean things up. Anthony Loke, leader of the Democratic Action Party—the largest party in Malaysia’s government— resigned from his post as transport minister in protest. It also seems at odds with the king’s own declaration in February, following another corruption scandal, that “no one is above the law.” So, why the clemency? Anwar has disavowed any responsibility, arguing that the power lies with the pardons board and ultimately the king. But critics still blame him. One factor at play is surely Najib’s ability to retain the vocal loyalty of hardcore supporters within his party who insist that “ Bossku ,” or their “boss,” is the victim of some elaborate witch hunt or is somehow the victim, and not the perpetrator, of fraud. His party, the United Malay National Organisation, is a key component of the current coalition government. In recent months, it has seen a revival of its political fortunes that would have been unthinkable a few years ago. The clemency seems extraordinary given the sheer scale of the corruption that Najib oversaw and the weight of evidence against him. Ahead of the meeting, analysts’ expectations of potential outcomes are limited , and experts who spoke to Foreign Policy said both parties believe a truce extension is in their interest. “Neither side wants to blow this up,” said Cory Combs, the head of critical mineral and supply chain research at Trivium China, a strategic advisory firm. * The West Is Sending Russia the Wrong Signals
  Foreign Policy | 22 Sep 20:46 UTC
  Europe’s fear of escalation is encouraging Putin.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Over four years after Russian President Vladimir Putin’s disastrous full-scale invasion of Ukraine, the West is still sending mixed messages to the Kremlin. Many Western leaders talk tough, arm Ukraine, impose sanctions, and warn Moscow against further aggression. Yet while Russia continues to immiserate Ukraine and Putin carries out an increasingly reckless campaign of provocations on NATO territory, those same governments—still struggling to wean themselves off of Russian oil and gas—often seem more worried about whether their response might be considered escalatory than about Russia’s own actions. This signals to Putin that he has plenty more runway for an asymmetric campaign of terror in Europe. Still, Putin has miscalculated the resolve of his adversaries before, such as in his expected three-day “special military operation” to conquer Ukraine, and he may again miscalculate the limits of NATO’s tolerance. The accretion of sub-threshold attacks may, when added together, end up crossing a threshold for more aggressive collective defense. Some estimates of the combined scale of Russian sabotage across Europe reveal over a hundred attacks, including potentially devastating ones in Germany last month, and the trajectory is increasing. A better strategy to reverse this trend would clarify concrete costs for Putin up front: Real red lines are usually identified only in hindsight. * How AI Fits Into Humanity’s Troubling History
  Foreign Policy | 22 Sep 18:46 UTC
  Like nuclear weapons, AI was born of human ingenuity. But its challenges are unique.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For anyone who has been paying attention to the news for the last week or so, with its saturated coverage of the dystopian powers of artificial intelligence, it would be easy to succumb to the conclusion that we are all doomed. Despite the fact that the coverage has been more heat than light, this take might actually be right. But that is not necessarily because AI will end up wiping out our species. Rather, it is because of something deeper and far more impervious to regulation: human nature itself. For anyone who has been paying attention to the news for the last week or so, with its saturated coverage of the dystopian powers of artificial intelligence, it would be easy to succumb to the conclusion that we are all doomed. Despite the fact that the coverage has been more heat than light, this take might actually be right. But that is not necessarily because AI will end up wiping out our species. Rather, it is because of something deeper and far more impervious to regulation: human nature itself. For years, I have taught courses in public policy in both the United States and China that explore the dark unexpected consequences of human ingenuity, and the pattern is clear. Many of our most consequential breakthroughs—meaning the things that help sustain notions of human progress—bring about vastly more complicated challenges in their wake. To help establish this picture, I often go back to the dawn of human society, when small bands of roving hunter-gatherers made the conceptual breakthrough that life might be less “nasty, brutish, and short,” to use Thomas Hobbes’s famous formula, if they instead selected good locations to settle down. The first of these are often thought to have been in the Tigris and Euphrates valley, as well as other river deltas, where predictable seasonal flooding and silt-laden soils favored the emergence of agriculture. Undoubtedly, this achievement was one of the greatest milestones in the human story. Over time, the food supply became more regular; surpluses were produced, priming an explosion in trade; and writing and record-keeping emerged, going hand in hand with the emergence of the first states. It was not long, though, before these seeming advancements wrought annihilation. As the late political scientist James Scott and many others have noted, entire early city-states were wiped out by the very fruits of this “progress.” As humans began living in dense communities, devastating outbr
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/22/artificial-intelligence-human-history-technological-development-risks/

Earlier this month, U.S. President Donald Trump unveiled his vision for new “next generation” U.S. Space Force uniforms to critical reviews. Derek Guy, for example, the menswear guy on X, quipped , “This is the kind of design costume designers make to tell you who are the bad guys in a space movie.” The sartorial criticism, however, cloaks a deeper and much more important deliberation about the future of America’s space strategy. Last week, the U.S. government revealed for the first time that it had deployed weapons in space. Air Force Secretary Troy Meink said the U.S. had fielded “on-orbit space control weapons capable of defending the Joint Force against hostile adversary action.” The announcement has already led to expressions of concern from some experts, but, in fact, Washington is right to deploy weapons in space. I recently participated in a debate, convened by the Aerospace Corporation, on whether the United States should embrace or avoid space warfare. I embraced the embrace position Of course, it would be wonderful if nations could cooperate and avoid competition and conflict, in space and other domains. But, alas, we are too late for that. The militarization of space is underway, and the United States and its allies must prepare to defend their interests and, ultimately, maintain the peace. Space is already weaponized. Since the 1950s, the major powers have possessed weapons, like intercontinental ballistic missiles (ICBMs), designed to travel through space. Since the 1960s, they have also utilized space-based assets, such as the Missile Defense Alarm System, or MIDAS, and Corona surveillance satellites, to assist their terrestrial military activities. The Pentagon’s earthly military activities, in particular, are very dependent on space-based assets for command, control, computing, communications, intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance (C4ISR). * Japan’s New CIA Could Be Takaichi’s Trump Card
  Foreign Policy | 22 Sep 13:21 UTC
  After decades of reticence, Tokyo is expanding its intelligence gathering abroad.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. When Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi meets with U.S. President Donald Trump on the sidelines of the U.N. General Assembly today, she will have a new plan at her disposal to highlight how Tokyo will do more for the U.S.-Japan security alliance. Her government is undertaking the most substantial overhaul of Japan’s intelligence collection and analysis capabilities since 1945, with the ultimate goal of creating something Japan has never had: its own version of the CIA by 2028. Although its final name and structure remain unsettled, Tokyo clearly intends for the new agency to conduct clandestine human intelligence (HUMINT) operations overseas against top security threats such as China, North Korea, and Russia. Since the end of World War II, Japan has conspicuously lacked a dedicated foreign intelligence service capable of systematically recruiting and running human sources overseas, in part because of the legacy of intelligence abuses during the imperial era. Yet Japan is hardly an intelligence novice. It has sophisticated technical collection capabilities, particularly in signals and geospatial intelligence. But technology cannot answer every question. Alberta is hurtling toward a controversial Oct. 19 independence referendum, with reported meddling by both U.S. influencers and Russian bot farms. But while this noisy sideshow distracts policymakers and pundits, a much more serious challenge to Canadian unity has arisen in the country’s far north, where the nation’s Inuit leadership has decried what one leader called “a very large shift” in the federal government’s dealings in the region. In the past few decades, modern treaties signed across Inuit Nunangat—the Inuit homeland that spans parts of Yukon, the Northwest Territories, Nunavut, Quebec, and Labrador—have affirmed the right for Inuit organizations to play a role in dictating resource and infrastructure developments across the region, which is home to vast strategic reserves of minerals used in green and military technologies. In some cases, that extends to outright ownership of the lands and resources in question; in others, treaties grant broad powers to reject developments that infringe upon traditional hunting and gathering activities. Under the leadership of former Prime Minister Justin Trudeau, treaty organizations such as Inuit Tapiriit Kanatami (ITK), the representative body for all Inuit in Canada, were often called upon for consultations—and respected for their views. * Explainer: How sea level rise poses an ‘existential threat’ to humans, heritage and nature
  Carbon Brief | 22 Sep 15:14 UTC
  For millions of people around the world, rising sea levels are already reshaping economies, livelihoods and cultures. The world’s oceans… The post Explainer: How sea level rise poses an ‘existential threat’ to humans, heritage and nature appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: For millions of people around the world, rising sea levels are already reshaping economies, livelihoods and cultures. The world’s oceans are currently rising at a faster rate than at any time in at least the past three millennia, with human influence the “ dominant cause ” of sea level rise since at least 1970. At the UN general assembly in New York this week, world leaders are set to adopt a high-level declaration on the “existential threats” posed by sea level rise. The declaration notes: “Sea level rise is not a distant scenario, but a real and lived experience for many.” On average, global sea levels rose by 20 centimetres (cm) between 1901 and 2018. This is due to both the melting of glaciers and ice sheets and the expansion of seawater as it warms, as well as changes in land-water storage . The rate of the rise has accelerated in recent years, with ocean levels rising 10.6cm since 1993. Sea level rise can vary locally due to seismic and volcanic activity, groundwater extraction and changes to the Earth’s surface resulting from ice melt. Under a moderate-emissions scenario , scientists predict that global average sea level will rise an additional 56cm by 2100, relative to a 1995-2014 baseline. Here, Carbon Brief unpacks some of the key ways that sea level rise threatens both societies and ecosystems. Around 770 million people – 10% of the world’s population – are at “acute risk” of negative impacts from sea level rise, according to a report from the UN secretary general released last month. (The report defines locations at acute risk as those that are less than five metres above the high-tide line.) The people at risk include the residents of several of the world’s largest cities , including Mumbai and Kolkata in India and Shenzhen and Guangzhou in China. It also encompasses the entire populations of many island nations. (See: Small island developing states .) There are two ways to consider sea level rise. Global-average sea level rise is the amount the ocean surface has moved upwards, on average, relative to a baseline. Relative, or local, sea level rise, is how much the ocean has risen in a given place. This can vary from the global average due to a number of factors, including land motion and ocean circulation, as well as changes to the Earth’s surface, rotation and gravitational pull due to the melting of the ice sheets. Higher sea levels bring with them myriad dangers for coastal communities: they can increase persistent flooding and inundation, 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/explainer-how-sea-level-rise-poses-an-existential-threat-to-humans-heritage-and-nature

* Analysis: Nepal’s $20m ‘loss-and-damage’ claim only covers 0.7% of flood costs
  Carbon Brief | 22 Sep 14:43 UTC
  The Nepali government has requested $20m from the UN fund for “loss and damage”, following the devastating floods that hit… The post Analysis: Nepal’s $20m ‘loss-and-damage’ claim only covers 0.7% of flood costs appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The Nepali government has requested $20m from the UN fund for “ loss and damage ”, following the devastating floods that hit the country in late August. This amounts to just 0.7% of the $2.7bn it has estimated in “physical damage” to infrastructure and “economic losses” from the flooding, according to Carbon Brief analysis. The $20m request would, nevertheless, make Nepal the fourth-largest claimant to the UN loss-and-damage fund, which is intended to help deal with climate-related disasters. Developing countries have already made 176 applications to the UN fund for responding to loss and damage , requesting, in total, $2.8bn to help recover from a variety of damaging events, such as drought, floods and storms. Yet, to date, predominantly developed nations have only pledged $0.8bn to support the fund. This means requests to the fund are already three times larger than the total amount pledged, raising questions about its potential to respond to emergencies. As yet, no funding has been paid out of the fund to developing countries grappling with intensifying and more frequent climate change-induced disasters since it was established in 2023. On 26 August, Nepal was hit by flash floods following a catastrophic rock-ice avalanche in the Himalayan border region. The flooding has killed more than 1,400 people, with thousands still missing . Days later, the Nepali government sought an “urgent response” from the board of the UN fund for climate-driven loss and damage. It subsequently requested $20m in “ financial compensation ” from the fund. This is the maximum amount that can currently be requested for a single project. “ Loss and damage ” is a term used to describe how climate change is causing serious and, in many cases, irreversible impacts around the world. Nations established the UN fund in 2023, after decades of effort by climate-vulnerable nations. Nepal’s national disaster management authority has now released an extensive rapid preliminary assessment of “damage and needs” from the floods. As the chart below shows, Nepal’s $20m loss-and-damage claim would only cover 0.7% of what authorities describe as $2.7bn in “total damage and loss” from the floods. When accounting for another $4.8bn in “recovery and reconstruction” costs – part of how loss and damage is internationally defined – then Nepal’s $20m claim would be just 0.3% of the needs it estimates.   https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-nepals-20m-loss-and-damage-claim-only-covers-0-7-of-flood-costs

  FULL TEXT: CME Group is adding Bitcoin Cash and Uniswap to its regulated futures lineup. The exchange said Tuesday it plans to launch BCH and UNI contracts on October 19, pending regulatory review. Traders get a choice between standard-sized contracts and smaller micro versions. Bitcoin Cash futures will represent 250 BCH, with micro contracts covering 25 BCH. Uniswap futures will cover 10,000 UNI, and its own micro contracts will trade at 1,000 UNI. Bitcoin Cash currently trades for $338 per coin, up more than 25% on the day, on a $6 billion market cap. Uniswap’s native token UNI meanwhile is changing hands at $9.28, up 5.5% in the last 24 hours, with a market cap of $5.75 billion. Bitcoin Cash split off from Bitcoin in 2017, built for a larger block size and cheaper payments rather than store-of-value use. Uniswap runs the largest decentralized exchange in crypto, and UNI works as the protocol's governance token—holders vote on upgrades rather than collect dividends. "As crypto markets continue to mature, participants require broader, regulated tools to navigate evolving digital asset related price risk," said Giovanni Vicioso, CME's global head of cryptocurrency products, in a statement. He described the new contracts as built for capital efficiency and exposure to "key crypto networks" inside a round-the-clock, regulated venue. BCH and UNI aren't CME's first altcoin bet this year. The exchange announced Avalanche and Sui futures in April, which launched May 4, following February's rollout of Cardano, Chainlink, and Stellar contracts. Those five, plus Bitcoin, Ethereum, XRP, and Solana, made up CME's crypto suite before Tuesday's announcement. That expansion is already paying off. CME said in its press release that its 2026 push into Cardano, Chainlink, Stellar, Avalanche, and Sui futures has generated more than $1 billion in total notional value year-to-date. Across the whole crypto complex, futures and options averaged 279,800 contracts daily in the first half of 2026—worth $8.3 billion in notional volume—with average open interest of 264,600 contracts, or $15.4 billion. Volatility Shares CEO and co-founder Justin Young called the addition "another important step in the continued expansion of CME Group's crypto futures offering," framing it as more tools for clients to "access these assets within a regulated futures framework."   FULL TEXT: Count the verbs in Alexander Chee’s essay, note the absence of emotional language, and then thank Annie Dillard for her part. Chee’s account of their semester together is an affectionate portrait that doubles as a brisk craft talk; in it, he puts Dillard’s lessons through their paces, a fitting tribute to the teacher who borrowed cigarette drags from her students, chewed through caramel candies during class, and nurtured his own artistic evolution with her rules and exercises. Narrative writing sets down details in an order that evokes the writer’s experience for the reader, she announced. This seemed obvious but also radical—no one had ever said it so plainly to us. She spoke often of “the job.” If you’re doing your job, the reader feels what you felt. You don’t have to tell the reader how to feel. No one likes to be told how to feel about something. And if you doubt that, just go ahead. Try and tell someone how to feel. One afternoon, at her direction, we brought in our pages, scissors and tape, and told to bring several drafts of an essay, one that we struggled with over many versions. Now cut out only the best sentences, she said. And tape them on a blank page. And then when you have that, write in around them, she said. Fill in what’s missing and make it reach for the best of what you’ve written thus far. “The writer and farmer on local knowledge, embracing limits, and the exploitation of rural America.” “Amy Wallace has spent two decades guarding the human her brother was—against a world that prefers David Foster Wallace as a puzzle.” “Why did James Whitehead—poet, fiction writer, and onetime college football player—fail to complete a successor to his celebrated first novel?”

  New Lines | 22 Sep 11:32 UTC
  In Jordan's Azraq oasis, farmers are struggling to survive as climate change and decades of groundwater depletion ruin their prospects. The post Water Scarcity in Jordan Is Causing an Agricultural Crisis appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: In Jordan, people remember retreating shorelines. From the oasis in Azraq to the Dead Sea in the Jordan Valley, bodies of water have been shrinking, leaving behind cratered land and shriveled shrubs in their wake. When asked about the former glory of their country’s wetland areas, residents are gripped by a powerful wistfulness. In the town of Azraq, 67 miles from the capital city of Amman, everyone who was born before the 1990s has a story to tell about the oasis that once was. Naif Alafeer used to wrangle large fish with his friends, Fadi Esaid used to cup his hands in the water and drink from them after hours of playing in the sun, and Shaira Alafeer used to dig with her fingers just beneath the surface to access the water that lurked there. Azraq — which means blue in Arabic — was the perfect moniker for the freshwater pools and springs that lapped at one’s feet. In the early 20th century, when residents initially settled in the area, they bought arable land because the brimming streams promised them perpetually irrigated farms and bountiful harvests. But I saw a vast, unending landscape before me. The ground cracked underfoot, a reminder of why Jordan ranks among the world’s most water-poor nations. Azraq’s story is a microcosm of the larger tragedy unfolding in the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan. Annual precipitation in the kingdom varies regionally between roughly 2 and 7.5 inches. Each person in Jordan is projected to have access to only 16,000 gallons of potable water annually. By comparison, 2024 data suggests that New York City’s 8.4 million residents had a per capita consumption of 118.19 gallons per person daily, which puts their annual consumption at roughly 43,139 gallons. Jordan relies on a public water system that supplies allotments of water once or twice a week to individual households. As a result, rooftop water tanks have become a permanent fixture of Jordan’s cityscapes. “A single distribution zone gets around 36 hours of water supply per week, but in the north, there are only six hours of water supply per week,” said Christian Klassert, a research associate who consults with Stanford University’s Jordan Water Project. While the country requires 1.4 billion cubic meters of water annually, it was already running a deficit of about 500 million cubic meters as of 2023. Conservative voters, angry about the water, electricity, and land demands of AI infrastructure such as data centers, say they are preparing to “ break rank .” The technology industry has discovered a basic political truth: People will not support an AI future if they believe its costs are being imposed on them while its benefits accrue elsewhere. In stark contrast, what little conversation is entertained in Washington focuses on narratives of “uncontrollable rogue agents” and loss of control. Consumer protection is too often treated as the mundane cousin of AI safety. Meanwhile, lawsuits accumulate on harmful manipulation, AI toys marketed to children surface explicit content, commonly available AI models easily allow nonconsensual deepfakes, and AI introduced in online maps amplifies misinformation. Effective consumer protections give users agency: the ability to know when they are interacting with an AI system, understand when AI has generated or altered information, challenge consequential automated decisions, and seek redress when things go wrong. Absent these protections, younger “digital native” Americans are clear : They will not willingly purchase or use these products. There is an uncomfortable geopolitical irony here. That phrase, magic mushrooms, usually calls to mind one type of mushroom: those containing psilocybin, the compound showing promise as a therapy for depression, addiction and other psychopathologies. But these gummies were not psilocybin. Instead, the packaging described Amanita muscaria , the red-capped, white-spotted toadstool of fairy-tale forests, iPhone keyboards and Super Mario. I knew a little about the folklore of Amanita muscaria . I had first seen it discussed in an episode of the show Hamilton’s Pharmacopeia (2016-21), which begins with a claim I now hear often: that Amanita muscaria may be among the oldest human intoxicants. The New York banker-turned-ethnomycologist R Gordon Wasson, whose Life magazine essay from 1957 introduced psilocybin mushrooms to a mass Western audience, certainly took this idea seriously. In his book Soma: Divine Mushroom of Immortality (1968), he argued that the Vedic soma – a ritual drink in ancient Indian religions – was made from Amanita muscaria . The mushroom has also been linked, sometimes carefully and sometimes wildly, to the origins of Santa Claus , Viking berserkers and even Christianity. Some believe its other name, ‘fly agaric’, stems from its use as a natural pesticide for house flies; the word muscaria is also derived from the Latin word musca , meaning fly. Some scholars have linked Amanita muscaria to Lewis Carroll’s mushroom scene in Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland (1865), in which the Caterpillar tells Alice that ‘one side’ of the mushroom will make her taller and the other shorter, though Carroll does not identify a species. From the 1922 edition of Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland , illustrated by Gwynedd M Hudson. I am a public-health scientist, and my questions were pragmatic: what exactly was in this package? Could it hurt someone? Were these products legal? The first thing I did was what I usually do. I went to the scientific literature. And down the rabbit hole I tumbled. * How Uganda Became a Holding Ground for America’s Deportees
  New Lines | 21 Sep 15:20 UTC
  They arrived in Uganda in handcuffs, broke and unsure where they were. A secret deportation deal between Uganda and the U.S. sent eight deportees halfway around the world, in possible violation of Ugandan, U.S. and international law. The post How Uganda Became a Holding Ground for America’s Deportees appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: On April 1, 2026, after a 16-hour journey to East Africa, an aircraft carrying eight deportees from the United States landed at Entebbe, a windswept peninsula in Uganda, on the northern shore of Lake Victoria. On the day of their arrival, mild sunshine and light showers beat a gentle rhythm across the town, which bears the lasting traces of the country’s colonial administration and hosts the official residence of the president’s family, squatting atop a lush green hill adjacent to the airport. The eight people, a woman and seven men, barely knew each other beyond their shared harrowing experience of having been expelled from the United States and placed on a flight to an unknown destination after being held for days in American detention centers, according to Robert Okot, a human rights lawyer based in Uganda who spoke to the deportees on two occasions. A group of human rights organizations asked Okot to offer legal services to the deportees, who spoke with him at the hotel. Most of the deportees hardly understood English, Uganda’s primary official language. They were mostly French-speaking, except one who spoke passable English. Okot relied on him for interpretation. “They wore filthy clothes and had no money or means of contacting their loved ones,” Okot told the Center for Collaborative Investigative Journalism (CCIJ), a partner in the investigative consortium behind the Deportation Project, led by Forbidden Stories and including New Lines . When they disembarked from the plane, the men were in handcuffs and leg chains, and were unshackled before the group was driven to a hotel in Mengo, a hilly suburb overlooking Kampala city, he said. “Bringing them to a country like Uganda, where people are struggling to find decent work and where they are depending on handouts from people for a meal, was quite traumatic,” he said. “They had not been told what they were being taken for and had no idea where they were, and did not have means of communication and cash to spend.” Okot added that they looked gaunt and haggard. The deportation and resulting trauma were the result of an Asylum Cooperative Agreement that the Ugandan government signed with the United States in July 2025. The agreement seeks to ensure the “dignified, safe, and timely transfer from the United States of America to the Republic of Uganda of third-country nationals present in the United States of America who may seek protection against the return to their home country or country of former habitua
  https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/how-uganda-became-a-holding-ground-for-americas-deportees/

The tomatoes do not have opinions, or so I tell myself, though it is mostly a lie. For they do lean toward the sun and then, perversely, away from it. They sulk when I overwater and they sulk when I forget, and they have firm convictions about how much attention is too much. But whatever they want, they show me plainly. A leaning plant wants turning. A pale, unhappy one wants trimming. A bushy one wants care. There is no tone in it—no door slamming or softly clicking shut upstairs, no message that says fine in a way that is clearly not fine. When a tomato is unhappy with me, it tells me in a language I can actually answer. And it does tell me. Skip a few days—a work trip, a long weekend, some obligation that drags me out of the side yard—and they turn unruly, almost insolent. “The bed of Lake Superior is part museum, part graveyard.” “Nature’s networks, fluidity, and diversity are the keys to our future.”

* He was probably right
  Aeon | 21 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Cicero’s life and thought are a testament to the idea of probabilia: we should be confident in our beliefs, but never certain - by Massimo Pigliucci Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A statue of Cicero at the Capitoline Museums, Rome, Italy. Photo by Alessandra Benedetti/Corbis via Getty Images How tightly should you hold your beliefs? You face the question several times a week without noticing. Hold them too loosely and you don’t stand for anything, hold them too tightly and you become the sort of person no argument can reach. Most of us swing between the two and have no vocabulary for the ground in between. A Roman lawyer worked one out, under pressure far worse than anything we are likely to face, and it is still the best answer available. The time is early 45 BCE, the place a villa near Lucrine Lake, northwest of Naples, in southern Italy. One of the most fascinating and controversial figures in the entirety of Western history, Marcus Tullius Cicero, is finishing writing a book that will be known as the Academica , where he explains the nature of sceptical philosophy and why it is so crucial not just to the advancement of human knowledge, but to living a good life. It is a strange moment to retire into one’s villa to write. The Roman Republic, so beloved by Cicero, is about to be destroyed by aspiring autocrats: Julius Caesar, a longtime friend and now a political opponent, has declared himself dictator for life, a move that will soon lead to his assassination on the Ides of March 44 BCE. On the personal front, Cicero has recently divorced Terentia, his wife of 30 years, and a few months ago his beloved daughter Tullia died from complications following childbirth. You would think a man whose private and public worlds are collapsing so dramatically would hardly have the focus to write philosophy. Yet for Cicero such writing is a form of therapy, and the treatises he produces will influence medieval Christians, Renaissance authors, and the Founders of the American Republic. C icero managed all this because of his adopted philosophy, ‘Academic Skepticism’, so-called because it characterised, for the period from 266 to 90 BCE, the Academy originally founded by Plato. He used to be treated as a mere populariser, a gifted Roman relaying Greek ideas he did not really own, but scholars abandoned that uncharitable verdict decades ago as it became clear that Cicero argued and decided among his sources rather than simply transcribing them. He also built a Latin philosophical vocabulary from scratch, and much of the abstract terminology of European thought descends from his coinages. On the question that mattered most to his own school – wheth
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-good-sceptic-has-more-than-doubt-and-less-than-certainty

It was complete rubbish, and they knew it,” Burke says. Burke is particularly interested in the rise of a secular, leftist form of political violence with its origins in the Middle East in the late 1960s. * The case for a robot tax to redistribute wealth
  Rest of World | 18 Sep 10:00 UTC
  In an excerpt from his book, “Innovate for Impact: A Roadmap to Sustainable Technology Beyond AI,” Alessandro Crimi explains that a tax on automation is more effective than retraining labor.
  FULL TEXT: AI has the potential to revolutionize economies by opening up new avenues for innovation, production, education, and problem solving. It also has the potential to increase current disparities and inequalities , and exacerbate environmental issues if it is not addressed critically. Integrating AI-driven innovation with social ideals that place a high priority on shared wealth, a just society, and ecological balance is a very difficult task. Retraining programs are a necessary but insufficient response to systemic displacement. A critical warning is being sounded that automation risks displacing labor and depressing wages unless addressed with safety nets and reallocation programs. Even in medieval times, which are rarely considered times of innovation, we had some sort of policies to reallocate people. The guild system, churches, and monasteries were training centers. Innovations like the windmill, or the watermill, created a demand for millwrights, who trained assistants informally. However, there is a reason why we remember medieval times as bleak; even with the retraining of people, innovations did not translate into improved living standards for the majority. Therefore, it is worth focusing on retraining policies, as well as profit sharing , taxation, and social safety nets as potential solutions for AI’s disruptions. With AI, although potential compensatory mechanisms such as new tasks and productivity gains are promised, history cautions us that such benefits are neither automatic nor equitably shared. The conversation around AI and labor, therefore, must broaden from a singular focus on retraining displaced workers to encompassing strategies for redistributing the wealth that automation creates, and interrogating the physical substrate upon which this new economy is built. Retraining programs are a necessary but insufficient response to systemic displacement. They place the full burden of adaptation on the individual and often fail to match the velocity of change. Structural policies that transform economic relationships are required to complement them, such as safety nets, reduction of working hours or universal basic income policies. The four-day workweek policy reduces the standard working week (usually to 32 hours) without cutting pay, aiming to improve the balance between work and home, productivity and mental health. UBI is a policy in which every adult receives a regular unconditional cash payment from the state to guarantee basic economic sec
  https://restofworld.org/2026/robot-tax-ai-wealth-redistribution/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* Cosmic amnesia
  Aeon | 18 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A black hole rings like a struck bell, then settles into silence, forgetting almost everything about its history. Why? - by Richard Dyer Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Artist’s impression of GW250114, the celestial collision of two black holes, observed in gravitational waves by the US National Science Foundation LIGO. Courtesy Aurore Simonnet (SSU/EdEon)/LVK/URI/LIGO/CALTECH It is 9 am on a warm July Sunday in Cambridge, as I head towards a café in the city centre. The bells of Great St Mary’s church play the familiar melody of the Cambridge Quarters . The sound, now often associated with the chimes of Big Ben in London, was first composed here in 1793. The quarters, named for the melody at each quarter-hour, are an immediately recognisable reminder of the passage of time. Each bell produces a note: a wave with a characteristic frequency that travels through the air and reaches our ears. A hammer strikes the bell, and it oscillates, pushing air molecules back and forth. Those jostling molecules reach our ears and we hear a musical note. Then the bell settles, gives up its energy, and returns to silence. My research here at the University of Cambridge often brings me back to the ringing of a bell. I don’t study the physics of sound, but I do focus on a remarkably similar process, taking place throughout the Universe. I am interested in another object that also rings: a black hole. As I sit down with a coffee, I open my laptop to a wavy signal that gradually fades to a flat line. Two black holes have finished merging – a frenzied process of coalescence – leaving an extremely brief wiggle originating from the remnant black hole. Much like the struck bell, this leftover black hole is ringing in a process called ringdown , and it’s consistent across every merger I investigate. Ringdown allows physicists to test their theories of gravity: did Albert Einstein really have the last word? Just as an off-sounding note from a bell might indicate a design flaw, a discrepancy in the frequencies of a ringing black hole could be the first crack in our understanding of gravity. That’s the scientific rationale for studying ringdown, but what fascinates me equally is what happens next. After the black hole rings, it settles into silence. In that moment, lasting only a few milliseconds, the power and intensity of the merger are lost. From the exterior, almost all of that history suddenly becomes inaccessible, and what is left is featureless. The black hole settles into a simple, peaceful object of pure spacetime, with no trace of the violence that just happened. In some respects, the black holes forget . It seems counterintuitive that some
  https://aeon.co/essays/black-holes-ring-like-bells-what-happens-next-is-stranger

  FULL TEXT: J.B. MacKinnon files a remarkable dispatch from among the members of the so-called Ostrich Army, who convened last fall in British Columbia to protest the planned culling of 300 or so birds following an H5N1 outbreak. The plight of the birds and the various claims made in their defense—that their antibodies could yield a cure for bird flu or COVID-19, for instance—are intriguing enough on their own. But it’s MacKinnon’s study of the Ostrich Army itself that helps this story take flight (even if the subjects at its center cannot). As helicopters pass overhead and a suspected kill pen mysteriously catches fire, suspicion mounts among the troops, and interpretations diverge. A genuinely surprising portrait of a loose, wayward movement, delivered with peak dramatic tension. This was the Ostrich Army way: every event called for interpretation, an attempt to see through to hidden endgames. “One thing with black magic,” one camper would later say to me, “is they tell you what they’re going to do before they do it.” And so, at first light, when firefighters had won the day and the smoke cleared to reveal the ostrich flock still standing, the sight didn’t lead to any reconsideration of the night’s theories of clandestine slaughter. The Army simply moved on, hoping to make better sense of the next move, or the one after that. The birds really could die this day. “Millions of songbirds in Southeast Asia are trapped and smuggled each year for keeping as pets or entering in competitions. Scientists warn that it’s fueling a crisis.” “As of May 2026, you can no longer buy or sell exotic cats in British Columbia. The letter’s condemnation of American-Israeli aggression leaves no ambiguity. * South Africa joins the global resistance against American data centers
  Rest of World | 17 Sep 10:00 UTC
  “A massive data center is trying to jump the queue and take our land, water, and energy.”
  FULL TEXT: Civil rights groups in South Africa are protesting the expansion of data centers by American companies, marking one of the first coordinated attempts on the continent to slow AI infrastructure development. “What we are saying is there should be a moratorium on data centers. … It must be across South Africa,” Kashiefa Achmat, chairperson of Housing Assembly, a Cape Town-based advocacy group, told Rest of World . South Africa has the most data centers on the continent, owned predominantly by American hyperscalers like Microsoft, Amazon, and Google, and data center operators such as Equinix and Digital Realty. They are racing to fill the continent’s rising demand for compute, which could hit 2.2 gigawatts by 2030, about five times the current levels, according to a McKinsey report. Microsoft has committed 5.4 billion rand ($300 million) by 2027 to expand its digital and AI infrastructure in South Africa. Amazon Web Services will invest $1.5 billion until 2029 to expand AI capabilities across Africa. In South Africa, Equinix plans to invest $438 million. “This is a strategic moment for African countries precisely because companies want access to new markets and locations,” Rachel Adams, founder of the Global Center on AI Governance, an African think tank on AI, told Rest of World . The expansion comes as data center projects globally face scrutiny over their environmental impact. In the U.S., residents have rallied against these facilities in Virginia, California, and Philadelphia. In June, New York imposed a statewide moratorium for up to a year on hyperscale data centers. Protests have also been seen elsewhere in the world, including by farmers in India and Indigenous groups in Brazil . Housing Assembly and U.K.-based nonprofit Foxglove filed a legal challenge in August against two data centers proposed by Equinix in Cape Town. Along with other rights groups, they called on the South African Human Rights Commission to investigate the projects’ impacts and impose a pause on hyperscale data center expansion. They have also called for new regulations requiring operators to disclose their water and electricity usage, and provide benefits to local communities. At the center of the protest is California-based Equinix, one of the world’s largest data center operators, which provides facilities to hyperscalers and other clients. It has benefited from the AI boom, with its market capitalization rising over 30% since last December to $99.36 billion as of September 16.

The post Somaliland’s Up...
  FULL TEXT: Among the world leaders coming to New York this month for the U.N. General Assembly, Abdirahman Mohamed Abdullahi occupies a peculiar place. His country’s flag does not fly from the masts on the plaza outside the U.N. headquarters in Manhattan, nor does his delegation receive diplomatic security. Yet Somaliland, the country he leads, is very real. This statelet of some 6 million people has sought recognition from the outside world since 1991, when it broke away from Somalia, so far to no avail. That changed last December, when Israel became the first country to offer recognition. Israel’s alleged motive for the recognition was to gain a military foothold on the Gulf of Aden, just across from the Houthis; but this did not prevent Somalilanders from seeing the moment as a turning point in their history. At last, an influential state was recognizing Somaliland’s decades-long efforts to build a stable state and a functioning democracy, as well as the strategic importance of its location overlooking the Bab al-Mandab Strait, one of the world’s major trade routes. Now, Abdullahi, known colloquially as “Irro,” is coming for the ultimate prize: U.S. recognition. That outcome is far from certain. The Somaliland delegation knows this, so it’s taking plenty of time to make its case. Abdullahi arrived in the U.S. on Sept. 5 and will spend the entire month in New York and Washington. “Our goal is to meet as many lawmakers and top officials as possible. The stakes for us are very high,” said Mohammed Abdirahman, director general of Somaliland’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs and International Cooperation. Abdullahi’s team is being deliberately vague about the “top officials”: They are hoping to secure a meeting with Secretary of State Marco Rubio or President Donald Trump but are still not sure they will succeed. The long-shot meeting has been in the works for months. Abdullahi met Eric Trump in Davos last January and has several influential friends in Washington, from Israeli diplomats and lobbyists to senior Republican lawmakers such as Sen. * The meaning of fake marble
  Aeon | 16 Sep 10:01 UTC
  From ancient Roman frescoes to modern kitchen benchtops, our desire to recreate marble is about more than cutting costs - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Humans have prized marble for its colours, patterns, texture and heft for thousands of years. In this video, Mary McGillivray, a digital creator and PhD student at the University of Melbourne, explores how, for as long as we’ve prized it, we’ve been inventing ways to fake it. McGillivray traverses Europe to investigate how, from ancient Rome to modern home-improvement stores, we’ve developed techniques to cast, plaster and paint imitations of the metamorphic rock. And, as McGillivray argues, this process has been about more than finding a cheap alternative to a luxury material. Rather, the millennia-long history of our fascination with marble, and of our attempts to recreate it, has much to say about how humanity is inspired by, and competes with, the natural world. From Latin America to Southeast and Central Asia, countries are buying American chips while adopting Chinese open-source models, splitting key AI projects between companies from two AI superpowers. The trend presents a growing complication for Washington’s competition with Beijing. Even countries investing heavily in American AI infrastructure are not necessarily choosing American technology for every layer of the AI stack. “Many countries around the world, particularly in the Global South, want the best of both worlds,” Kyle Chan, a fellow at the John L.
